Well, I came in because for a while now, I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. Which, by the way, the crossover between the arts and finance here is actually really fascinating. A lot of people don't realize that Cincinnati has this massive philanthropic backbone—my grandfather actually used to talk about the old manufacturing money that built the Music Hall. He was a machinist, completely different world from what I do as a financial advisor, but he just loved the architecture downtown. He used to take me on these walking tours when I was a kid. 

Anyway, um... what was I saying? Right, the tracking. It's just that they keep sending me little signals through things like invitations, or the specific topics chosen for panels, or even who sits near me at these events. And, I mean, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence... I just can’t really let it go.

Well, yes, I do think they're capable of it. I mean, these are very well-connected people, you know? They have the resources. And it's not like I haven't thought about it logically. I... I think it’s just me. I mean, I haven't seen anyone else reacting to the invitations or the panel topics the way I do. It really feels like they are specifically tracking *my* clients, *my* meetings. Which is why the signals feel so directed at me, you know? Charity dinners are such a production anyway. You have to rent the tux, and the rental places always smell like mothballs and dry cleaning fluid. I went to this one place on Main Street, and the guy running it—older guy, maybe in his sixties—he spent twenty minutes telling me about how the fabric of tuxedo lapels has changed since the 1980s. He had this whole collection of vintage jackets in the back. It was actually pretty interesting, but I was running late to meet a client for coffee. We usually go to this little spot by the river... 

Um... sorry, I lost my train of thought again. Right, the signals. Yeah, I’ve tried to tell myself it’s a coincidence, but I just can't let it go. It really feels like it’s just me they’re doing this to.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean, like I was saying before... it’s definitely about me. I know I keep repeating myself, but it’s just so tailored. The panel topics or who they sit me next to, it always relates back to a client I just met with. It’s their way of showing me they’re tracking my movements. 

Like, they sat me at this table last month that had these awful acrylic ghost chairs. Have you seen those? They’re completely clear plastic, supposed to look modern, I guess. My old roommate in college bought a set of them because he thought it made our apartment look bigger. He was an architecture major at UC, always obsessed with spatial perception and light. We lived in this tiny place in Clifton, and honestly, no amount of clear plastic was going to make that shoebox feel like a penthouse. The heating was terrible, too. We used to just walk down to this late-night pizza place on the corner just to stay warm near their ovens... 

Um, sorry. I lost my train of thought again. But yeah, the seating arrangements and the panel topics... even when I try to tell myself it's just a coincidence, I really can't let it go. It's all a signal, and it's definitely directed at me.

He was an architecture major at UC, always obsessed with spatial perception and light. We lived in this tiny place in Clifton, and honestly, no amount of clear plastic was going to make that shoebox feel like a penthouse. The heating was terrible, too. We used to just walk down to this late-night pizza place on the corner just to stay warm near their ovens... \n\nUm, sorry. I lost my train of thought again. But yeah, the seating arrangements and the panel topics... even when I try to tell myself it's just a coincidence, I really can't let it go. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean... I guess part of me knows that's a possibility? Like I said, I do try to tell myself it could just be a coincidence, or maybe my mind playing tricks on me. But I just can't really let it go. It feels too real. It's like I've been convinced for a while now that these people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they're sending me these little signals through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events. 

Cincinnati has such a weird arts scene, anyway. It's very insular. I was at this gallery opening in Over-the-Rhine a few weeks ago, and they were serving these tiny little crostinis with some kind of fig jam. The gallery owner was wearing this ridiculous oversized scarf, even though it was completely sweltering in there because the AC was broken. I don't know why people wear scarves indoors. My uncle used to do that, but he lived in Chicago, so at least there it made sense. He was a huge Cubs fan. He actually caught a foul ball once back in the 90s...

Wait, sorry. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Oh, right, if it's in my mind. I try to think logically about it, but no, I really believe they are sending me these messages. I just can't shake it.

And they're sending me these little signals through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events. \n\nCincinnati has such a weird arts scene, anyway. It's very insular. I was at this gallery opening in Over-the-Rhine a few weeks ago, and they were serving these tiny little crostinis with some kind of fig jam. The gallery owner was wearing this ridiculous oversized scarf, even though it was completely sweltering in there because the AC was broken. I don't know why people wear scarves indoors. My uncle used to do that, but he lived in Chicago, so at least there it made sense. He was a huge Cubs fan. He actually caught a foul ball once back in the 90s...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Oh, right, if it's in my mind. I try to think logically about it, but no, I really believe they are sending me these messages. I just can't shake it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these signals you believe are being sent to you affected your daily routine or work as a financial advisor?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / personal_financial_advisor / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still working, but my practice has gotten smaller because I miss follow-ups, avoid some public panels, and have trouble keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'm still working. I mean, I haven't quit or anything. But... yeah, my practice has definitely gotten smaller. I just find myself missing follow-ups with people, and I've started avoiding some of the public panels and networking events where I know they'll be. It's just hard to keep up the same level of client contact I used to manage when I'm constantly trying to decode everything.

Speaking of networking events, they always have those terrible little nametags, you know? The ones with the adhesive that leaves a sticky residue on your jacket lapel. I ruined a really nice navy blazer that way once. I had to take it to this dry cleaner over in Hyde Park. The woman who runs it is amazing, she got the stain right out. She has this fat orange cat that just sleeps on the counter all day. I love cats, but I'm allergic. My sister had a cat growing up, a Siamese, and it was so mean. It used to hide under the stairs and swipe at my ankles when I walked by...

Um, sorry. What was I saying? Right, my work. Yeah, it's definitely affecting things. I just can't do the follow-ups and the events like I used to. It's just too much.

I guess part of me knows that's a possibility? Like I said, I do try to tell myself it could just be a coincidence, or maybe my mind playing tricks on me. But I just can't really let it go. The ones with the adhesive that leaves a sticky residue on your jacket lapel. I ruined a really nice navy blazer that way once. I had to take it to this dry cleaner over in Hyde Park. The woman who runs it is amazing, she got the stain right out. She has this fat orange cat that just sleeps on the counter all day. I love cats, but I'm allergic. My sister had a cat growing up, a Siamese, and it was so mean. It used to hide under the stairs and swipe at my ankles when I walked by...\n\nUm, sorry. What was I saying? Right, my work. Yeah, it's definitely affecting things. I just can't do the follow-ups and the events like I used to. It's just too much.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think there's any way to stop these people from tracking you, or is it something you feel you have to live with?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / personal_financial_advisor / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't really know if there's a way to stop it. I mean, I've tried to just ignore it, but they're so persistent. It feels like something I just have to deal with, you know? Unless I completely uproot my life and move away. I've actually thought about moving to Seattle. I have a buddy who lives out there, works for Amazon or Microsoft, one of those big tech places. He says it rains a lot, but honestly, I don't mind the rain. It's kind of calming. 

I bought this really nice umbrella a couple of years ago, one of those ones with the wooden hook handle. It's British, I think. Supposed to withstand hurricane-force winds or something crazy like that. Not that we get hurricanes in Ohio, but the wind off the river can get pretty brutal in the winter. Last January, I was walking across the Roebling Bridge and the wind was so strong it practically blew my glasses right off my face. I had to hold onto the railing. Have you ever walked across that bridge? It's the one that looks like a mini Brooklyn Bridge. Same architect, actually. John Roebling. He built the one here first as a prototype. It's fascinating how they used to engineer things back then, without computers or anything, just math and cables...

Wait. Sorry. Did I even answer your question? I don't know how to stop them. I just... I try to avoid the events, but they always find a way to let me know they're watching. I guess I just have to live with it for now.

It feels too real. It's like I've been convinced for a while now that these people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they're sending me these little signals through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events. \n\nCincinnati has such a weird arts scene, anyway. It's very insular. I was at this gallery opening in Over-the-Rhine a few weeks ago, and they were serving these tiny little crostinis with some kind of fig jam. My uncle used to do that, but he lived in Chicago, so at least there it made sense. He was a huge Cubs fan. He actually caught a foul ball once back in the 90s...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Oh, right, if it's in my mind. I try to think logically about it, but no, I really believe they are sending me these messages. I mean, I haven't quit or anything. But... yeah, my practice has definitely gotten smaller. I just find myself missing follow-ups with people, and I've started avoiding some of the public panels and networking events where I know they'll be. It's just hard to keep up the same level of client contact I used to manage when I'm constantly trying to decode everything.\n\nSpeaking of networking events, they always have those terrible little nametags, you know? The ones with the adhesive that leaves a sticky residue on your jacket lapel. I ruined a really nice navy blazer that way once. I had to take it to this dry cleaner over in Hyde Park. The woman who runs it is amazing, she got the stain right out. She has this fat orange cat that just sleeps on the counter all day. I love cats, but I'm allergic. My sister had a cat growing up, a Siamese, and it was so mean. It used to hide under the stairs and swipe at my ankles when I walked by...\n\nUm, sorry. What was I saying? Right, my work. Yeah, it's definitely affecting things. I just can't do the follow-ups and the events like I used to. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any way to stop these people from tracking you, or is it something you feel you have to live with?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if there's a way to stop it. I mean, I've tried to just ignore it, but they're so persistent. It feels like something I just have to deal with, you know? Unless I completely uproot my life and move away. I've actually thought about moving to Seattle. I have a buddy who lives out there, works for Amazon or Microsoft, one of those big tech places. He says it rains a lot, but honestly, I don't mind the rain. It's kind of calming. \n\nI bought this really nice umbrella a couple of years ago, one of those ones with the wooden hook handle. It's British, I think. Supposed to withstand hurricane-force winds or something crazy like that. Not that we get hurricanes in Ohio, but the wind off the river can get pretty brutal in the winter. Last January, I was walking across the Roebling Bridge and the wind was so strong it practically blew my glasses right off my face. I had to hold onto the railing. Have you ever walked across that bridge? It's the one that looks like a mini Brooklyn Bridge. Same architect, actually. John Roebling. He built the one here first as a prototype. It's fascinating how they used to engineer things back then, without computers or anything, just math and cables...\n\nWait. Sorry. Did I even answer your question? I don't know how to stop them. I just... I try to avoid the events, but they always find a way to let me know they're watching. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, I don't know if it's one specific message, like a single sentence or something. But it's definitely a message that they know what I'm doing. Like I said, I've been convinced for a while now that these people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they send me these little signals to show me they know. It's in the invitations they send, the panel topics they choose, even who sits near me at these events. 

Like, last month, I went to this panel on emerging markets, and the guy sitting next to me kept tapping his pen. Click, click, click. It was one of those nice Cross pens, you know? My dad had one of those. He got it for his 25th work anniversary at the insurance company. It came in this little velvet box. He never actually used it, just kept it on his desk. He was always so careful with his things. He used to polish his shoes every Sunday night while watching 60 Minutes. I can still smell the shoe polish. It has that distinct, kind of chemical smell. Reminds me of when I used to play baseball in Little League and we had to oil our gloves. I played second base. I wasn't very good at hitting, but I had a decent glove...

Oh, I'm sorry. I lost my train of thought again. The message... right. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I just can't really let it go. They want me to know they're watching who I meet with. That's the message.

It causes a lot of anxiety, honestly. That's actually why I came in to see you. Because for a while now, I’ve been so convinced that these certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they keep sending me those little signals I mentioned, through the invitations, the panel topics, even who sits near me at events. And the hardest part is, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. It just eats at me. 

It makes me so tense that sometimes I try to go to the Cincinnati Art Museum just to clear my head. They have this beautiful wing with local pottery and ceramics. My grandmother used to collect pottery. She had these really heavy clay bowls from New Mexico, all turquoise and terracotta. We used to eat cereal out of them when we visited her in the summers. She lived in this tiny house that always smelled like dried lavender and old paper. She was a librarian, actually. She worked at the public library for forty years and completely refused to use the computerized catalog system when they installed it in the nineties. She kept her own little card catalog in a shoebox on her desk. She said computers were going to ruin our memories. She might have been right about that, actually, considering how much time everyone spends staring at their phones now... 

Um, sorry. I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Oh, the distress. Right. Yeah, it's just... even when I try to rationalize it, I can't let it go. So I'm pretty stressed out about it every single day.

He never actually used it, just kept it on his desk. He was always so careful with his things. He used to polish his shoes every Sunday night while watching 60 Minutes. I can still smell the shoe polish. It has that distinct, kind of chemical smell. Reminds me of when I used to play baseball in Little League and we had to oil our gloves. I played second base. I wasn't very good at hitting, but I had a decent glove...\n\nOh, I'm sorry. I lost my train of thought again. The message... right. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I just can't really let it go. They want me to know they're watching who I meet with. That's the message.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel on a daily basis because of these signals you believe are being sent to you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of anxiety, honestly. That's actually why I came in to see you. Because for a while now, I’ve been so convinced that these certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they keep sending me those little signals I mentioned, through the invitations, the panel topics, even who sits near me at events. And the hardest part is, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. It just eats at me. \n\nIt makes me so tense that sometimes I try to go to the Cincinnati Art Museum just to clear my head. They have this beautiful wing with local pottery and ceramics. My grandmother used to collect pottery. She had these really heavy clay bowls from New Mexico, all turquoise and terracotta. We used to eat cereal out of them when we visited her in the summers. She lived in this tiny house that always smelled like dried lavender and old paper. She was a librarian, actually. She worked at the public library for forty years and completely refused to use the computerized catalog system when they installed it in the nineties. She kept her own little card catalog in a shoebox on her desk. She said computers were going to ruin our memories. She might have been right about that, actually, considering how much time everyone spends staring at their phones now... \n\nUm, sorry. I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Oh, the distress. Right. Yeah, it's just... even when I try to rationalize it, I can't let it go. So I'm pretty stressed out about it every single day.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think it's possible that you might be misinterpreting these signals and that there's a more rational explanation for what you're experiencing?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / personal_financial_advisor / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending me little signals through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events, and even when I tell myself it could just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, I try to tell myself that. I really do. I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, that there's a rational explanation. But I just can't really let it go. I'm still convinced that these people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they're sending me those little signals through the invitations, the panel topics, who sits near me at events. 

It's like... how many coincidences can there be before it's a pattern, right? Speaking of patterns, I've been trying to learn how to knit lately. My sister gave me a bunch of yarn for my birthday. She thought it would help with my stress. It's actually pretty hard to keep the tension right on the yarn. You have to hold it a certain way. She made me this incredible scarf last winter. It was mustard yellow, which sounds ugly, but it actually looks really good with my navy peacoat. I bought that coat at a vintage shop in Over-the-Rhine. The guy who runs the shop is a real character, always wears these bright velvet bow ties. He told me the coat belonged to a sailor in the 1950s. I don't know if I believe him, but it's a good story. I love finding things with a history, you know? It makes you wonder who wore it before you, what kind of life they had...

Wait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Going off track. But yeah, to answer your question... I try to think it's just a coincidence, but I just can't shake the feeling that it's real. I can't let it go.

Yeah, it's definitely affecting things. I just can't do the follow-ups and the events like I used to. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any way to stop these people from tracking you, or is it something you feel you have to live with?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if there's a way to stop it. He says it rains a lot, but honestly, I don't mind the rain. It's kind of calming. \n\nI bought this really nice umbrella a couple of years ago, one of those ones with the wooden hook handle. I'm not really sure if it's a huge problem, but I guess I do wander off a bit. Like I just did. I try my best to stay focused, especially at work. You really have to pay attention when you're managing people's retirement portfolios. 

Speaking of retirement, I was just looking at this brochure for a community down in Florida for one of my older clients. It had all these pictures of people playing pickleball. They make a really good oatmeal stout there, actually. It's got this heavy coffee finish. I usually prefer lighter beers, but in the winter, a stout is nice... 

Wait, sorry. Yeah. I guess I do lose my focus during conversations. My brain just sort of jumps from one thing to the next because everything reminds me of something else, you know? But I don't know if it's always difficult for me or if it's just kind of how I talk.

Yeah, it's definitely affecting things. I just can't do the follow-ups and the events like I used to. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any way to stop these people from tracking you, or is it something you feel you have to live with?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if there's a way to stop it. He says it rains a lot, but honestly, I don't mind the rain. It's kind of calming. \n\nI bought this really nice umbrella a couple of years ago, one of those ones with the wooden hook handle. I just... I try to avoid the events, but they always find a way to let me know they're watching. I guess I just have to live with it for now.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people tracking you are trying to communicate a specific message to you through these signals?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if it's one specific message, like a single sentence or something. He never actually used it, just kept it on his desk. He was always so careful with his things. He used to polish his shoes every Sunday night while watching 60 Minutes. I mean, I haven't seen anyone else reacting to the invitations or the panel topics the way I do. It really feels like they are specifically tracking *my* clients, *my* meetings. Which is why the signals feel so directed at me, you know? Like they know exactly who I sat down with on Tuesday and then by Thursday, there's a nod to it in the seating chart at a charity dinner. \n\nCharity dinners are such a production anyway. You have to rent the tux, and the rental places always smell like mothballs and dry cleaning fluid. I went to this one place on Main Street, and the guy running it—older guy, maybe in his sixties—he spent twenty minutes telling me about how the fabric of tuxedo lapels has changed since the 1980s. He had this whole collection of vintage jackets in the back. It was actually pretty interesting, but I was running late to meet a client for coffee. We usually go to this little spot by the river... \n\nUm... sorry, I lost my train of thought again. Right, the signals. Yeah, I’ve tried to tell myself it’s a coincidence, but I just can't let it go. It really feels like it’s just me they’re doing this to.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the signals you're receiving, such as the panel topics or seating arrangements, are specifically about you or related to you in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, like I was saying before... it’s definitely about me. I know I keep repeating myself, but it’s just so tailored. The panel topics or who they sit me next to, it always relates back to a client I just met with. It’s their way of showing me they’re tracking my movements. \n\nLike, they sat me at this table last month that had these awful acrylic ghost chairs. Have you seen those? They’re completely clear plastic, supposed to look modern, I guess. My old roommate in college bought a set of them because he thought it made our apartment look bigger. He was an architecture major at UC, always obsessed with spatial perception and light. We lived in this tiny place in Clifton, and honestly, no amount of clear plastic was going to make that shoebox feel like a penthouse. The heating was terrible, too. We used to just walk down to this late-night pizza place on the corner just to stay warm near their ovens... \n\nUm, sorry. I lost my train of thought again. But yeah, the seating arrangements and the panel topics... even when I try to tell myself it's just a coincidence, I really can't let it go. It's all a signal, and it's definitely directed at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these signals you're receiving could be a product of your own mind rather than actual messages from others?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess part of me knows that's a possibility? Like I said, I do try to tell myself it could just be a coincidence, or maybe my mind playing tricks on me. But I just can't really let it go. It feels too real. It's like I've been convinced for a while now that these people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they're sending me these little signals through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events. \n\nCincinnati has such a weird arts scene, anyway. It's very insular. I was at this gallery opening in Over-the-Rhine a few weeks ago, and they were serving these tiny little crostinis with some kind of fig jam. The gallery owner was wearing this ridiculous oversized scarf, even though it was completely sweltering in there because the AC was broken. I don't know why people wear scarves indoors. My uncle used to do that, but he lived in Chicago, so at least there it made sense. He was a huge Cubs fan. He actually caught a foul ball once back in the 90s...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Oh, right, if it's in my mind. I try to think logically about it, but no, I really believe they are sending me these messages. I just can't shake it.\n\nDoctor: Have these signals you believe are being sent to you affected your daily routine or work as a financial advisor?\n\nPatient: I'm still working. I mean, I haven't quit or anything. But... yeah, my practice has definitely gotten smaller. I just find myself missing follow-ups with people, and I've started avoiding some of the public panels and networking events where I know they'll be. It's just hard to keep up the same level of client contact I used to manage when I'm constantly trying to decode everything.\n\nSpeaking of networking events, they always have those terrible little nametags, you know? The ones with the adhesive that leaves a sticky residue on your jacket lapel. I ruined a really nice navy blazer that way once. I had to take it to this dry cleaner over in Hyde Park. The woman who runs it is amazing, she got the stain right out. She has this fat orange cat that just sleeps on the counter all day. I love cats, but I'm allergic. My sister had a cat growing up, a Siamese, and it was so mean. It used to hide under the stairs and swipe at my ankles when I walked by...\n\nUm, sorry. What was I saying? Right, my work. Yeah, it's definitely affecting things. I just can't do the follow-ups and the events like I used to. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any way to stop these people from tracking you, or is it something you feel you have to live with?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if there's a way to stop it. I mean, I've tried to just ignore it, but they're so persistent. It feels like something I just have to deal with, you know? Unless I completely uproot my life and move away. I've actually thought about moving to Seattle. I have a buddy who lives out there, works for Amazon or Microsoft, one of those big tech places. He says it rains a lot, but honestly, I don't mind the rain. It's kind of calming. \n\nI bought this really nice umbrella a couple of years ago, one of those ones with the wooden hook handle. It's British, I think. Supposed to withstand hurricane-force winds or something crazy like that. Not that we get hurricanes in Ohio, but the wind off the river can get pretty brutal in the winter. Last January, I was walking across the Roebling Bridge and the wind was so strong it practically blew my glasses right off my face. I had to hold onto the railing. Have you ever walked across that bridge? It's the one that looks like a mini Brooklyn Bridge. Same architect, actually. John Roebling. He built the one here first as a prototype. It's fascinating how they used to engineer things back then, without computers or anything, just math and cables...\n\nWait. Sorry. Did I even answer your question? I don't know how to stop them. I just... I try to avoid the events, but they always find a way to let me know they're watching. I guess I just have to live with it for now.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people tracking you are trying to communicate a specific message to you through these signals?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if it's one specific message, like a single sentence or something. Like I said, I've been convinced for a while now that these people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I just can't really let it go. They want me to know they're watching who I meet with. That's actually why I came in to see you. And they keep sending me those little signals I mentioned, through the invitations, the panel topics, even who sits near me at events. And the hardest part is, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. It just eats at me. \n\nIt makes me so tense that sometimes I try to go to the Cincinnati Art Museum just to clear my head. They have this beautiful wing with local pottery and ceramics. My grandmother used to collect pottery. She had these really heavy clay bowls from New Mexico, all turquoise and terracotta. We used to eat cereal out of them when we visited her in the summers. She lived in this tiny house that always smelled like dried lavender and old paper. She was a librarian, actually. She worked at the public library for forty years and completely refused to use the computerized catalog system when they installed it in the nineties. She kept her own little card catalog in a shoebox on her desk. She said computers were going to ruin our memories. She might have been right about that, actually, considering how much time everyone spends staring at their phones now... \n\nUm, sorry. I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Oh, the distress. Right. Yeah, it's just... even when I try to rationalize it, I can't let it go. I don't know if it's hard to keep my thoughts in order, really. I mean, I guess I just talk the way I talk. Like, my thoughts just kind of flow together. It's like a stream, you know? What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because for a while now, I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. Which, by the way, the crossover between the arts and finance here is actually really fascinating. A lot of people don't realize that Cincinnati has this massive philanthropic backbone—my grandfather actually used to talk about the old manufacturing money that built the Music Hall. He was a machinist, completely different world from what I do as a financial advisor, but he just loved the architecture downtown. He used to take me on these walking tours when I was a kid. \n\nAnyway, um... what was I saying? Right, the tracking. It's just that they keep sending me little signals through things like invitations, or the specific topics chosen for panels, or even who sits near me at these events. And, I mean, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence... I just can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people are actually capable of tracking you in the way you've described?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I do think they're capable of it. I mean, these are very well-connected people, you know? They have the resources. And it's not like I haven't thought about it logically. Like I said, I tell myself it could just be a coincidence—that maybe the panel topic on wealth distribution just happened to align with a portfolio I was reviewing that morning, or the seating arrangement at the gala was just random. But I just can't really let it go. It feels too specific. \n\nAnd speaking of galas, the catering at the last one was just abysmal. They tried to do this fusion menu, but it was just a mess. My sister, she's a chef actually, over in Covington, and she always says that if you're going to do fusion, you have to respect the base ingredients. She makes this incredible Thai-basil risotto. Yeah, I just... I really believe they are keeping tabs on who I'm meeting with. I mean, I haven't seen anyone else reacting to the invitations or the panel topics the way I do. It really feels like they are specifically tracking *my* clients, *my* meetings. Which is why the signals feel so directed at me, you know? You have to rent the tux, and the rental places always smell like mothballs and dry cleaning fluid. I went to this one place on Main Street, and the guy running it—older guy, maybe in his sixties—he spent twenty minutes telling me about how the fabric of tuxedo lapels has changed since the 1980s. We usually go to this little spot by the river... \n\nUm... sorry, I lost my train of thought again. Right, the signals. Yeah, I’ve tried to tell myself it’s a coincidence, but I just can't let it go. It really feels like it’s just me they’re doing this to.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the signals you're receiving, such as the panel topics or seating arrangements, are specifically about you or related to you in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, like I was saying before... it’s definitely about me. I know I keep repeating myself, but it’s just so tailored. The panel topics or who they sit me next to, it always relates back to a client I just met with. It’s their way of showing me they’re tracking my movements. \n\nLike, they sat me at this table last month that had these awful acrylic ghost chairs. Have you seen those? They’re completely clear plastic, supposed to look modern, I guess. My old roommate in college bought a set of them because he thought it made our apartment look bigger. He was an architecture major at UC, always obsessed with spatial perception and light. We lived in this tiny place in Clifton, and honestly, no amount of clear plastic was going to make that shoebox feel like a penthouse. The heating was terrible, too. We used to just walk down to this late-night pizza place on the corner just to stay warm near their ovens... \n\nUm, sorry. I lost my train of thought again. But yeah, the seating arrangements and the panel topics... even when I try to tell myself it's just a coincidence, I really can't let it go. It's all a signal, and it's definitely directed at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these signals you're receiving could be a product of your own mind rather than actual messages from others?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess part of me knows that's a possibility? Like I said, I do try to tell myself it could just be a coincidence, or maybe my mind playing tricks on me. But I just can't really let it go. It feels too real. It's like I've been convinced for a while now that these people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they're sending me these little signals through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events. \n\nCincinnati has such a weird arts scene, anyway. It's very insular. I was at this gallery opening in Over-the-Rhine a few weeks ago, and they were serving these tiny little crostinis with some kind of fig jam. The gallery owner was wearing this ridiculous oversized scarf, even though it was completely sweltering in there because the AC was broken. I don't know why people wear scarves indoors. My uncle used to do that, but he lived in Chicago, so at least there it made sense. He was a huge Cubs fan. He actually caught a foul ball once back in the 90s...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Oh, right, if it's in my mind. I try to think logically about it, but no, I really believe they are sending me these messages. I just can't shake it.\n\nDoctor: Have these signals you believe are being sent to you affected your daily routine or work as a financial advisor?\n\nPatient: I'm still working. I mean, I haven't quit or anything. But... yeah, my practice has definitely gotten smaller. I just find myself missing follow-ups with people, and I've started avoiding some of the public panels and networking events where I know they'll be. It's just hard to keep up the same level of client contact I used to manage when I'm constantly trying to decode everything.\n\nSpeaking of networking events, they always have those terrible little nametags, you know? The ones with the adhesive that leaves a sticky residue on your jacket lapel. I ruined a really nice navy blazer that way once. I had to take it to this dry cleaner over in Hyde Park. The woman who runs it is amazing, she got the stain right out. She has this fat orange cat that just sleeps on the counter all day. I love cats, but I'm allergic. My sister had a cat growing up, a Siamese, and it was so mean. It used to hide under the stairs and swipe at my ankles when I walked by...\n\nUm, sorry. What was I saying? Right, my work. Yeah, it's definitely affecting things. I just can't do the follow-ups and the events like I used to. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any way to stop these people from tracking you, or is it something you feel you have to live with?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if there's a way to stop it. I mean, I've tried to just ignore it, but they're so persistent. It feels like something I just have to deal with, you know? Unless I completely uproot my life and move away. I've actually thought about moving to Seattle. I have a buddy who lives out there, works for Amazon or Microsoft, one of those big tech places. He says it rains a lot, but honestly, I don't mind the rain. It's kind of calming. \n\nI bought this really nice umbrella a couple of years ago, one of those ones with the wooden hook handle. It's British, I think. Supposed to withstand hurricane-force winds or something crazy like that. Not that we get hurricanes in Ohio, but the wind off the river can get pretty brutal in the winter. Last January, I was walking across the Roebling Bridge and the wind was so strong it practically blew my glasses right off my face. I had to hold onto the railing. Have you ever walked across that bridge? It's the one that looks like a mini Brooklyn Bridge. Same architect, actually. John Roebling. He built the one here first as a prototype. It's fascinating how they used to engineer things back then, without computers or anything, just math and cables...\n\nWait. Sorry. Did I even answer your question? I don't know how to stop them. I just... I try to avoid the events, but they always find a way to let me know they're watching. I guess I just have to live with it for now.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people tracking you are trying to communicate a specific message to you through these signals?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if it's one specific message, like a single sentence or something. But it's definitely a message that they know what I'm doing. Like I said, I've been convinced for a while now that these people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they send me these little signals to show me they know. It's in the invitations they send, the panel topics they choose, even who sits near me at these events. \n\nLike, last month, I went to this panel on emerging markets, and the guy sitting next to me kept tapping his pen. Click, click, click. It was one of those nice Cross pens, you know? My dad had one of those. He got it for his 25th work anniversary at the insurance company. It came in this little velvet box. He never actually used it, just kept it on his desk. He was always so careful with his things. He used to polish his shoes every Sunday night while watching 60 Minutes. I can still smell the shoe polish. It has that distinct, kind of chemical smell. Reminds me of when I used to play baseball in Little League and we had to oil our gloves. I played second base. I wasn't very good at hitting, but I had a decent glove...\n\nOh, I'm sorry. I lost my train of thought again. The message... right. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I just can't really let it go. They want me to know they're watching who I meet with. That's the message.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel on a daily basis because of these signals you believe are being sent to you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of anxiety, honestly. That's actually why I came in to see you. Because for a while now, I’ve been so convinced that these certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they keep sending me those little signals I mentioned, through the invitations, the panel topics, even who sits near me at events. And the hardest part is, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. It just eats at me. \n\nIt makes me so tense that sometimes I try to go to the Cincinnati Art Museum just to clear my head. They have this beautiful wing with local pottery and ceramics. My grandmother used to collect pottery. She had these really heavy clay bowls from New Mexico, all turquoise and terracotta. We used to eat cereal out of them when we visited her in the summers. She lived in this tiny house that always smelled like dried lavender and old paper. She was a librarian, actually. She worked at the public library for forty years and completely refused to use the computerized catalog system when they installed it in the nineties. She kept her own little card catalog in a shoebox on her desk. She said computers were going to ruin our memories. She might have been right about that, actually, considering how much time everyone spends staring at their phones now... \n\nUm, sorry. I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Oh, the distress. Right. Yeah, it's just... even when I try to rationalize it, I can't let it go. So I'm pretty stressed out about it every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be misinterpreting these signals and that there's a more rational explanation for what you're experiencing?\n\nPatient: I mean, I try to tell myself that. I really do. I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, that there's a rational explanation. But I just can't really let it go. I'm still convinced that these people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they're sending me those little signals through the invitations, the panel topics, who sits near me at events. \n\nIt's like... how many coincidences can there be before it's a pattern, right? Speaking of patterns, I've been trying to learn how to knit lately. My sister gave me a bunch of yarn for my birthday. She thought it would help with my stress. It's actually pretty hard to keep the tension right on the yarn. You have to hold it a certain way. She made me this incredible scarf last winter. It was mustard yellow, which sounds ugly, but it actually looks really good with my navy peacoat. I bought that coat at a vintage shop in Over-the-Rhine. The guy who runs the shop is a real character, always wears these bright velvet bow ties. He told me the coat belonged to a sailor in the 1950s. I don't know if I believe him, but it's a good story. I love finding things with a history, you know? It makes you wonder who wore it before you, what kind of life they had...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Going off track. But yeah, to answer your question... I try to think it's just a coincidence, but I just can't shake the feeling that it's real. I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused on one topic during conversations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if it's a huge problem, but I guess I do wander off a bit. Like I just did. I try my best to stay focused, especially at work. You really have to pay attention when you're managing people's retirement portfolios. \n\nSpeaking of retirement, I was just looking at this brochure for a community down in Florida for one of my older clients. It had all these pictures of people playing pickleball. Have you noticed how huge pickleball is getting? They actually just paved over the old tennis courts near my apartment to make room for it. The sound of those plastic balls hitting the paddles is so loud, just this constant sharp *thwack, thwack*. It echoes right off the brick of the old brewery building across the street. They make a really good oatmeal stout there, actually. It's got this heavy coffee finish. I usually prefer lighter beers, but in the winter, a stout is nice... \n\nWait, sorry. Yeah. I guess I do lose my focus during conversations. My brain just sort of jumps from one thing to the next because everything reminds me of something else, you know? But I don't know if it's always difficult for me or if it's just kind of how I talk.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family since you started believing you're being tracked?\n\nPatient: I guess... yeah, things have changed a bit with friends and family. I mean, I still get my work done, just in pieces, you know? But I don't really have the same spark I used to. Like, I'll just leave client follow-ups sitting there on my desk. My desk is actually this really nice mahogany one I got at an estate sale. The guy who owned it before was some kind of architect in the seventies. It has these secret little compartments in the drawers, which I thought was so cool when I bought it. I used to keep my nice pens in there. I have this one fountain pen my dad gave me when I graduated college. It's got a gold nib and writes so smoothly. You have to buy this special ink for it in these little glass bottles. The ink is called \"Midnight Blue,\" but it honestly looks more like a dark purple when it dries on the paper...\n\nOh, right, my friends. Yeah, so... I just don't have the energy for them right now. I skip the jazz nights we used to go to, or the pickup basketball games on the weekends. And I don't really even feel bad about missing them, which is weird. And when my friends ask what's going on or why I'm not around, I just give them really short answers. Honestly, just talking and trying to explain it all feels like way more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I don't know if it's hard to keep my thoughts in order, really. I mean, I guess I just talk the way I talk. Like, my thoughts just kind of flow together. It's like a stream, you know? There's this little creek that runs behind my parents' house in the suburbs. When we were kids, my brother and I used to build these little dams out of rocks and mud to see if we could stop the water. We'd spend hours out there, getting completely covered in dirt. My mom would make us hose off in the driveway before we were allowed back in the house. She had this really beautiful garden right next to the driveway, full of these bright pink hydrangeas. She always said the secret to getting them that color was putting coffee grounds in the soil. I tried doing that with a fern I bought for my apartment, but it just ended up attracting fruit flies. Those little flies are impossible to get rid of once they're in your kitchen. You have to make these traps with apple cider vinegar and dish soap... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Keeping my thoughts in order? Yeah, I don't really know. I just kind of say what comes to mind. I haven't really noticed it being a problem, honestly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And they're sending me those little signals through the invitations, the panel topics, who sits near me at events. \n\nIt's like... how many coincidences can there be before it's a pattern, right? Speaking of patterns, I've been trying to learn how to knit lately. My sister gave me a bunch of yarn for my birthday. She thought it would help with my stress. It's actually pretty hard to keep the tension right on the yarn. You have to hold it a certain way. She made me this incredible scarf last winter. It was mustard yellow, which sounds ugly, but it actually looks really good with my navy peacoat. I bought that coat at a vintage shop in Over-the-Rhine. The guy who runs the shop is a real character, always wears these bright velvet bow ties. He told me the coat belonged to a sailor in the 1950s. I don't know if I believe him, but it's a good story. I love finding things with a history, you know? It makes you wonder who wore it before you, what kind of life they had...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Going off track. But yeah, to answer your question... I try to think it's just a coincidence, but I just can't shake the feeling that it's real. I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused on one topic during conversations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if it's a huge problem, but I guess I do wander off a bit. You really have to pay attention when you're managing people's retirement portfolios. \n\nSpeaking of retirement, I was just looking at this brochure for a community down in Florida for one of my older clients. The sound of those plastic balls hitting the paddles is so loud, just this constant sharp *thwack, thwack*. It echoes right off the brick of the old brewery building across the street. They make a really good oatmeal stout there, actually. It's got this heavy coffee finish. I usually prefer lighter beers, but in the winter, a stout is nice... \n\nWait, sorry. Yeah. I guess I do lose my focus during conversations. My brain just sort of jumps from one thing to the next because everything reminds me of something else, you know? But I don't know if it's always difficult for me or if it's just kind of how I talk.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family since you started believing you're being tracked?\n\nPatient: I guess... yeah, things have changed a bit with friends and family. It's fascinating how they used to engineer things back then, without computers or anything, just math and cables...\n\nWait. Sorry. Did I even answer your question? I don't know how to stop them. I just... I try to avoid the events, but they always find a way to let me know they're watching. I guess I just have to live with it for now.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people tracking you are trying to communicate a specific message to you through these signals?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if it's one specific message, like a single sentence or something. But it's definitely a message that they know what I'm doing. Like I said, I've been convinced for a while now that these people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they send me these little signals to show me they know. It's in the invitations they send, the panel topics they choose, even who sits near me at these events. \n\nLike, last month, I went to this panel on emerging markets, and the guy sitting next to me kept tapping his pen. Click, click, click. It was one of those nice Cross pens, you know? My dad had one of those. He got it for his 25th work anniversary at the insurance company. It came in this little velvet box. He never actually used it, just kept it on his desk. He was always so careful with his things. He used to polish his shoes every Sunday night while watching 60 Minutes. I can still smell the shoe polish. It has that distinct, kind of chemical smell. Reminds me of when I used to play baseball in Little League and we had to oil our gloves. I played second base. I wasn't very good at hitting, but I had a decent glove...\n\nOh, I'm sorry. I lost my train of thought again. The message... right. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I just can't really let it go. They want me to know they're watching who I meet with. That's the message.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel on a daily basis because of these signals you believe are being sent to you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of anxiety, honestly. That's actually why I came in to see you. Because for a while now, I’ve been so convinced that these certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they keep sending me those little signals I mentioned, through the invitations, the panel topics, even who sits near me at events. And the hardest part is, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. It just eats at me. \n\nIt makes me so tense that sometimes I try to go to the Cincinnati Art Museum just to clear my head. They have this beautiful wing with local pottery and ceramics. My grandmother used to collect pottery. She had these really heavy clay bowls from New Mexico, all turquoise and terracotta. We used to eat cereal out of them when we visited her in the summers. She lived in this tiny house that always smelled like dried lavender and old paper. She was a librarian, actually. She worked at the public library for forty years and completely refused to use the computerized catalog system when they installed it in the nineties. She kept her own little card catalog in a shoebox on her desk. She said computers were going to ruin our memories. She might have been right about that, actually, considering how much time everyone spends staring at their phones now... \n\nUm, sorry. I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Oh, the distress. Right. Yeah, it's just... even when I try to rationalize it, I can't let it go. So I'm pretty stressed out about it every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be misinterpreting these signals and that there's a more rational explanation for what you're experiencing?\n\nPatient: I mean, I try to tell myself that. I really do. I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, that there's a rational explanation. But I just can't really let it go. I'm still convinced that these people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And they're sending me those little signals through the invitations, the panel topics, who sits near me at events. \n\nIt's like... how many coincidences can there be before it's a pattern, right? Speaking of patterns, I've been trying to learn how to knit lately. My sister gave me a bunch of yarn for my birthday. She thought it would help with my stress. It's actually pretty hard to keep the tension right on the yarn. You have to hold it a certain way. She made me this incredible scarf last winter. It was mustard yellow, which sounds ugly, but it actually looks really good with my navy peacoat. I bought that coat at a vintage shop in Over-the-Rhine. The guy who runs the shop is a real character, always wears these bright velvet bow ties. He told me the coat belonged to a sailor in the 1950s. I don't know if I believe him, but it's a good story. I love finding things with a history, you know? It makes you wonder who wore it before you, what kind of life they had...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Going off track. But yeah, to answer your question... I try to think it's just a coincidence, but I just can't shake the feeling that it's real. I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused on one topic during conversations?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if it's a huge problem, but I guess I do wander off a bit. Like I just did. I try my best to stay focused, especially at work. You really have to pay attention when you're managing people's retirement portfolios. \n\nSpeaking of retirement, I was just looking at this brochure for a community down in Florida for one of my older clients. It had all these pictures of people playing pickleball. Have you noticed how huge pickleball is getting? They actually just paved over the old tennis courts near my apartment to make room for it. The sound of those plastic balls hitting the paddles is so loud, just this constant sharp *thwack, thwack*. It echoes right off the brick of the old brewery building across the street. They make a really good oatmeal stout there, actually. It's got this heavy coffee finish. I usually prefer lighter beers, but in the winter, a stout is nice... \n\nWait, sorry. Yeah. I guess I do lose my focus during conversations. My brain just sort of jumps from one thing to the next because everything reminds me of something else, you know? But I don't know if it's always difficult for me or if it's just kind of how I talk.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family since you started believing you're being tracked?\n\nPatient: I guess... yeah, things have changed a bit with friends and family. I mean, I still get my work done, just in pieces, you know? But I don't really have the same spark I used to. Like, I'll just leave client follow-ups sitting there on my desk. My desk is actually this really nice mahogany one I got at an estate sale. The guy who owned it before was some kind of architect in the seventies. It has these secret little compartments in the drawers, which I thought was so cool when I bought it. I used to keep my nice pens in there. I have this one fountain pen my dad gave me when I graduated college. It's got a gold nib and writes so smoothly. You have to buy this special ink for it in these little glass bottles. The ink is called \"Midnight Blue,\" but it honestly looks more like a dark purple when it dries on the paper...\n\nOh, right, my friends. Yeah, so... I just don't have the energy for them right now. I skip the jazz nights we used to go to, or the pickup basketball games on the weekends. And I don't really even feel bad about missing them, which is weird. And when my friends ask what's going on or why I'm not around, I just give them really short answers. Honestly, just talking and trying to explain it all feels like way more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I don't know if it's hard to keep my thoughts in order, really. I mean, I guess I just talk the way I talk. Like, my thoughts just kind of flow together. It's like a stream, you know? There's this little creek that runs behind my parents' house in the suburbs. When we were kids, my brother and I used to build these little dams out of rocks and mud to see if we could stop the water. We'd spend hours out there, getting completely covered in dirt. My mom would make us hose off in the driveway before we were allowed back in the house. She had this really beautiful garden right next to the driveway, full of these bright pink hydrangeas. She always said the secret to getting them that color was putting coffee grounds in the soil. I tried doing that with a fern I bought for my apartment, but it just ended up attracting fruit flies. Those little flies are impossible to get rid of once they're in your kitchen. You have to make these traps with apple cider vinegar and dish soap... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Keeping my thoughts in order? Yeah, I don't really know. I just kind of say what comes to mind. I haven't really noticed it being a problem, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to maintain your current relationships or daily routines because of your concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm still working. I haven't lost my job or anything. But my practice has definitely gotten smaller. I just... I miss follow-ups, and I've been avoiding some of those public panels I used to do. It's just hard to keep up the same level of client contact I used to manage. Especially with the phone calls. I used to make all my calls on this old rotary phone I found at a flea market. It's this heavy, mint-green thing from the sixties. It took forever to dial a number, but I loved the mechanical sound it made. *Click-click-click-click-click*. I had to buy a special adapter to make it work with the digital lines in my office building. The guy who sold me the adapter had this tiny electronics shop crammed between a dry cleaner and a really good Thai restaurant. They made the best Pad See Ew there, with those wide, flat noodles. You know, the kind that get a little charred on the edges from the wok? I tried making it at home once, but I couldn't get the wok hot enough on my electric stove. Gas stoves are so much better for cooking, but my landlord won't let us have them because of some fire code thing...\n\nWait, sorry. Routines. Yeah, so, I'm just not doing as much as I used to. The clients, the panels... it's just harder to stay on top of it all right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Disconnected? Um, no, I don't really feel like that. Everything connects for me, actually. It's all part of the same picture. Like a puzzle, I guess. I used to do a lot of jigsaw puzzles to unwind after looking at client portfolios all day. I bought this massive 2,000-piece one a few years ago of a castle in Germany. Neuschwanstein, I think it's called? It's the one that inspired the Disney castle. Anyway, I spent almost three weeks working on it on my dining room table. I couldn't even eat dinner there, I had to eat on the couch and watch TV. I was watching this documentary series about deep-sea diving at the time. It's crazy how dark it gets down in the ocean, and the fish look like aliens with those little glowing lights on their heads. Bioluminescence, they call it. I read that some of them use it to attract prey, and others use it to communicate with each other in the pitch black... 

But, uh, sorry. Disconnected thoughts? No, I don't think they are. I mean, they make perfect sense to me when I'm talking.

I have this one fountain pen my dad gave me when I graduated college. It's got a gold nib and writes so smoothly. You have to buy this special ink for it in these little glass bottles. The ink is called \"Midnight Blue,\" but it honestly looks more like a dark purple when it dries on the paper...\n\nOh, right, my friends. Yeah, so... I just don't have the energy for them right now. I mean, I guess I just talk the way I talk. Like, my thoughts just kind of flow together. It's like a stream, you know? There's this little creek that runs behind my parents' house in the suburbs. When we were kids, my brother and I used to build these little dams out of rocks and mud to see if we could stop the water. We'd spend hours out there, getting completely covered in dirt. My mom would make us hose off in the driveway before we were allowed back in the house. She had this really beautiful garden right next to the driveway, full of these bright pink hydrangeas. She always said the secret to getting them that color was putting coffee grounds in the soil. I tried doing that with a fern I bought for my apartment, but it just ended up attracting fruit flies. It's just hard to keep up the same level of client contact I used to manage. Especially with the phone calls. I used to make all my calls on this old rotary phone I found at a flea market. It's this heavy, mint-green thing from the sixties. It took forever to dial a number, but I loved the mechanical sound it made. *Click-click-click-click-click*. I had to buy a special adapter to make it work with the digital lines in my office building. The guy who sold me the adapter had this tiny electronics shop crammed between a dry cleaner and a really good Thai restaurant. They made the best Pad See Ew there, with those wide, flat noodles. You know, the kind that get a little charred on the edges from the wok? I tried making it at home once, but I couldn't get the wok hot enough on my electric stove. Am I avoiding things because of the tracking? I mean... I am skipping things, but I don't know if it's just that. I just really don't have the same spark lately. Like, I'll skip jazz nights, or my regular pickup basketball games, and I just do it without much feeling about it either way.

They were supposed to make you jump higher too, but that's just pure marketing. Kind of like how they sell those mutual funds with the flashy prospectuses, but when you actually look at the expense ratios, they're just eating all your returns. I had a client a couple of years ago who insisted on buying into this obscure tech ETF just because the ticker symbol spelled out his dog's name. People get so emotional about their money, which is why they hire someone like me to look at the numbers objectively. You have to keep a clear head to do this job. I drink a lot of green tea for that, the matcha kind that comes in the little bamboo tins. You're supposed to whisk it in a special bowl, but I usually just shake it up in a travel mug... \n\nWait, sorry. Social interactions, right. Yeah, so I just skip the games and the jazz nights now. I still get my work done in pieces, but I'll leave a lot of client follow-ups just sitting there. And when my friends ask what's going on with me, I just give them really short answers. Honestly, just talking to people feels like way more effort than it should right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping between completely unrelated topics when you're speaking?\n\nPatient: Jumping between topics? I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, my thoughts are just flowing naturally, you know? Like a stream. I actually went fishing in a stream a few years ago up in the mountains. It was supposed to be this relaxing weekend trip, but the cabin we rented had a huge hornet's nest right above the front door. We had to go in and out through the back sliding glass door the whole time. The sliding door was super sticky on the tracks, like it hadn't been greased since the nineties. I tried using WD-40 on it, but it just made a mess on the carpet. WD-40 is weird stuff, you know? It's not actually a lubricant, it's a water displacer. I read that it was invented for the aerospace industry to stop missiles from rusting. It's crazy how much military technology ends up in our garages. Like duct tape, that was from World War II. They needed something waterproof to seal ammunition boxes... \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, unrelated topics. No, I don't think I do that. Everything I say makes sense to me when I'm saying it. It all just connects in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with your family and friends due to your belief that you're being tracked?\n\nPatient: Family and friends? I mean, a little, I guess. I'm just pulling back from everyone in general, honestly. Even at work. I’m still working, but my practice has actually gotten a lot smaller lately. I used to do these public financial panels to get new clients, usually at those big chain hotels downtown. The ones with the really aggressively patterned carpets. I read once that hotels and casinos use those crazy carpet patterns to hide stains, which makes sense, but it really messes with your eyes if you stare at it too long. At the last panel I did, they had this catering setup with the absolute worst coffee. It was in one of those big silver urns with the little spout, and it just tasted like burnt aluminum. I actually started roasting my own coffee beans at home because of stuff like that. You can buy green coffee beans in bulk online, and they look completely different, almost like little dried peas. I use an old hot-air popcorn popper to roast them out on my balcony. The chaff gets everywhere, though. It blows all over the neighbor's patio... \n\nBut, um, wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, relationships. Yeah, so I just avoid those public panels completely now. And I miss a lot of follow-ups. I'm just having a lot of trouble keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage. It's just too much to deal with right now."
